Under the high-dimensional regime where
d/L\rightarrow\xi
, we know that
d\lambda\rightarrow(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot\xi<1
, which can be regarded as a constant.
Thus,
\varrho_{t}
decreases and converges to
0^{+}
at a rate of
\Theta((d\sqrt{t})^{-1})
, i.e.,
\varrho_{\infty}\to 0^{+}
.
While
\varphi_{t}
increases at a rate of
\Theta(d\sqrt{t})
when
t
increases, and thus grows to infinity.
Moreover,
while
t
increases,
the product
2\varphi_{t}\varrho_{t}
remains almost constant and converges to
1/(1+(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot\xi)
during this phase.

In summary, through a heuristic two-timescale analysis, we prove that
\varrho_{t}
decreases to zero after reaching its peak.
And
\varphi_{t}
increases to infinity while maintaining
\varphi_{t}\varrho_{t}
at a constant level.

Appendix C
Implications of Learned Pattern of QK and OV Circuits

C.1
Transformer Model as a Sum of Kernel Regressors

Recall that we use
{\mathtt{smax}}(\cdot)
denote the softmax function and use
Z_{\mathsf{ebd}}\in\mathbb{R}^{(d+1)\times(L+1)}
denote
\begin{bmatrix}Z&z_{q}\end{bmatrix}
, where
Z
contains the first
L
demonstration samples and
z_{q}=(x_{q}^{\top},0)^{\top}
contains the test input.
Here the
L
demonstration samples are generated from a noisy linear model with parameter
\beta
, which itself is drawn from a distribution
\mathsf{P}_{\beta}
.
The transformer output
{\mathtt{TF}}_{\theta}(Z_{\mathsf{ebd}})
is specified in (
2.2
), which is a
(d+1)\times(L+1)
-dimensional matrix. Finally, the prediction head outputs the
(d+1,L+1)
-th entry of
{\mathtt{TF}}_{\theta}(Z_{\mathsf{ebd}})
, denoted as
\widehat{y}_{q}
, which is used to predict the desired response
\beta^{\top}x_{q}
. Notice that the
(d+1,L+1)
-th entry of
Z_{\mathsf{ebd}}
is the
(d+1)
-th entry of
z_{q}
, which is equal to zero.
Due to causal masking,
z
does not attend to itself and only attend to the previous tokens, namely
Z
.
Thus, we can simplify the
(L+1)
-th column of
{\mathtt{TF}}_{\theta}(Z_{\mathsf{ebd}})
to

\displaystyle\sum_{h=1}^{H}O^{(h)}V^{(h)}Z\cdot{\mathtt{smax}}(Z^{\top}K^{(h)^{\top}}Q^{(h)}z_{q}\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{(d+1)}.

(C.1)

Compared with (
2.2
), we replace
Z_{\mathsf{ebd}}
with
Z
in the value part of attention, because causal masking ensures that the output of the softmax function is a probability distribution over
[L]
. Then,
\widehat{y}_{q}
is the last entry of the vector in (
C.1
).
Under the simplified parametrization given in (
3.1
),
we have

Z^{\top}K^{(h)^{\top}}Q^{(h)}z_{q}=\omega^{(h)}\cdot Xx_{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{L},\qquad O^{(h)}V^{(h)}Z=\mu^{(h)}y\in\mathbb{R}^{L},\qquad\forall h\in[H].

Thus, we can simplify
\widehat{y}_{q}
to the following form:

\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{q}=\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\cdot\left\langle y,{\mathtt{smax}}\left(\omega^{(h)}\cdot Xx_{q}\right)\right\rangle:=\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\cdot y^{\top}p^{(h)},

(C.2)

where we define
p^{(h)}={\mathtt{smax}}\left({\omega^{(h)}}\cdot Xx_{q}\right)
for all
h\in[H]
.
Here,
p^{(h)}
and
\mu^{(h)}\cdot y
are the attention score and value of the
h
-th head at position
(L+1)
, respectively.

Interpretation of (
C.2
) as a Sum of Kernel Regressors.

Consider the
h
-th head, where
p^{(h)}
is a probability distribution over
[L]
, and the probability assigned to each
\ell
is proportional to
\exp(\omega^{(h)}\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})
. Then, the output of the
h
-th head is equal to

\displaystyle\mu^{(h)}\cdot\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.0738,0.4711}\ell}=1}^{L}y_{{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.0738,0.4711}\ell}}\cdot\mathfrak{K}(x_{q},x_{{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.0738,0.4711}\ell}};\omega^{(h)}),\qquad\textrm{where}\quad\mathfrak{K}(x_{q},x_{{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.0738,0.4711}\ell}};\omega)=\frac{\exp(\omega\cdot x_{{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.0738,0.4711}\ell}}^{\top}x_{q})}{\sum_{i=1}^{L}\exp(\omega\cdot x_{i}^{\top}x_{q})}.

(C.3)

Here
\mathfrak{K}(x_{q},x_{\ell};\omega)
is a kernel function that captures the similarity of
x_{q}
and
x_{\ell}
and the parameter
1/\omega
plays the role of bandwidth of the kernel.
Notice that the kernel
\mathfrak{K}
in (
C.3
) is slightly different from the standard Gaussian radial basis function (RBF) kernel, which is defined as
\exp(-\omega\cdot\|x_{q}-x_{\ell}\|_{2}^{2})
, where
\omega
is a parameter.
These two kernels coincide when when
x_{q}
and
x_{\ell}
are on the unit sphere.
However, in our case,
\mathsf{P}_{x}
is supported on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and thus these two kernels are different.
Nevertheless, each term as in (
C.3
) is still a Nadaraya-Watson kernel regression predictor.
Moreover, the intuition of such an estimator is clear: the output in (
C.3
) is a weighted sum of the responses
\{y_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[L]}
in the demonstration data, and the weights are determined by the similarity between the test input
x_{q}
and the demonstration input
x_{\ell}
.
In summary, when the parameters of the transformer are given by (
3.1
), the prediction
\widehat{y}_{q}
is a sum of
H
kernel regressors.

Interpretation of Multi-Head Attention Predictor with
(\omega,\mu)\subseteq\mathscr{S}_{\gamma}
.

As shown in the empirical observations in §
3
and the solution manifold in §
4.2
, parameters
\{(\omega^{(h)},\mu^{(h)})\}_{h\in[H]}
found by the transformer model are in the solution manifold
\mathscr{S}_{\gamma}
defined in (
4.7
) for some
\gamma>0
.
Thus,
\widehat{y}_{q}
in (
C.2
) can be simplified as a sum of two kernel regressors:

\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{q}=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.0738,0.4711}\ell}=1}^{L}\mu_{\gamma}\cdot y_{i}\cdot\bigl(\mathfrak{K}(x_{q},x_{{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.0738,0.4711}\ell}};\gamma)-\mathfrak{K}(x_{q},x_{{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.0738,0.4711}\ell}};-\gamma)\bigr).

(C.4)

The intuition behind such an estimator is as follows. The kernel
\mathfrak{K}(x_{q},x_{\ell};\gamma)
assigns a larger weight to
x_{\ell}
when

x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}
is large, i.e., when
x_{q}
and
x_{\ell}
are similar. The kernel
\mathfrak{K}(x_{q},x_{\ell};-\gamma)
is large when
x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}
is small, i.e., when
x_{q}
and
x_{\ell}
are dissimilar.
Thus, the predictor in (
C.4
) is a sum of two kernel regression predictors based on datasets
\{x_{\ell},y_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[L]}
and
\{-x_{\ell},-y_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[L]}
respectively.
It is also reasonable why both these two kernel regressors appear. Specifically,
(x_{\ell},y_{\ell})
and
(-x_{\ell},-y_{\ell})
have the same distribution when
x_{\ell}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
and
y_{\ell}
is generated from a linear model. Thus, we can use both
\{x_{\ell},y_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[L]}
and
\{-x_{\ell},-y_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[L]}
to learn the parameter
\beta
.

Difference between Single-Head and Multi-Head Softmax Attention.

As shown in
Chen et al. (2024a)
, when
H=1
, the learned transformer implements a Nadaraya-Watson kernel regressor as in (
C.3
), with
|\omega^{(1)}|\asymp 1/\sqrt{d}
.
Moreover, the KQ and OV matrices of the trained single-head model exhibit a positive or negative only pattern.
In contrast, when
H\geq 2
, the multi-head attention mechanism learns to behave as a sum of kernel regressors, achieving better performance due to its greater expressive power.
Furthermore,the trained multi-head model displays a coupled positive-negative pattern and works in a regime with a significantly smaller magnitudes of KQ parameters.
Therefore, despite the form of the sum of kernel regressors, the multi-head attention indeed learns to approximate a variant of the debiased GD predictor, which better captures the linear structure of the problem compared to the single-head case (see §
4.3
for details).

C.2
Proof of Proposition
4.1
: Loss Approximation

For better understanding the approximation and scaling conditions in Proposition
C.1
, we provide a more detailed discussion alongside the simulation results in §
A.4
.

Proposition C.1

(Formal Statement of Proposition
4.1
)
.

Under the parametrization in (
3.1
), consider the
H
-head attention model with dimension
d\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
and sample size
L\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.
Suppose that the parameters
(\omega,\mu)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2H}
satisfy one of the following conditions:

\|\omega\|_{\infty}\leq 0.1\sqrt{\log L/\max\{d,\log L\}},\qquad\max\{\|\mu\|_{\infty},\|\mu\|_{\infty}^{2}\}\lesssim L^{-\lambda/2+3/10},

where
\lambda>0
is an absolute constant defining the error level. Then, it holds that

\mathcal{L}(\omega,\mu)=1+\sigma^{2}-2\mu^{\top}\omega+\mu^{\top}\big(\omega\omega^{\top}+(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot L^{-1}\cdot\exp(d\omega\omega^{\top})\big)\mu+O(dH^{2}\cdot L^{-\lambda}).

Here
O(\cdot)
omits some universal constant.

The informal statement in Proposition
4.1
follows by assuming
d>\log L
and setting
\lambda=1/5
. A more refined trade-off between the scales of
\omega
and
\mu
can be achieved by carefully selecting
\kappa
, the scaling factor for
\omega
, i.e.,
\|\omega\|_{\infty}\leq\kappa\sqrt{\log L/\max\{d,\log L\}}
, where
\kappa
is now set to a constant 0.1.

Proof of Proposition
C.1
.

We prove this proposition in three steps:

•

In
Part 1
, we first expand the population loss
\mathcal{L}(\omega,\mu)
into multiple terms, where each term involves expectations over the randomness of both coefficient
\beta\sim\mathsf{P}_{\beta}
and ICL samples
Z_{\mathsf{ebd}}
.

•

In
Part 2
, we simplify these terms by conditioning on
x_{q}
and taking an expectation over the randomness of the rest
\beta
and
\{(x_{\ell},y_{\ell})\}_{\ell\in[L]}
within the context sequence, which gives us a reformulation of the loss in terms of
\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}
and
moments
of the attention probabilities.

•

In
Part 3
, we isolate the high-order moments from the low-order moments in the reformulated loss. We show that low-order terms can be well approximated by a polynomial of OV weights and exponential QK weights up to
O(dH\cdot L^{-\lambda})
error, and high-order terms can also be uniformly bounded by
O(dH^{2}\cdot L^{-\lambda})
under appropriate scaling conditions.

Step 1. Expand Population Loss into Multiple Terms.

Recall that under the simplified parametrization given in (
3.1
), the prediction of the transformer model,
\widehat{y}_{q}
, is given by (
C.2
), where we let
p^{(h)}={\mathtt{smax}}(\omega^{(h)}\cdot Xx_{q})
be the attention score of the
h
-th head.
Then, the population loss defined in (
2.5
) can be expanded as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\omega,\mu)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\beta^{\top}x_{q}-\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\cdot(X\beta+\epsilon)^{\top}p^{(h)}\biggr)^{2}\biggr]+\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\Bigl[(\beta^{\top}x_{q})^{2}\Bigr]}_{\displaystyle\textbf{(i)}}-2\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\biggl[\beta^{\top}x_{q}\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}(X\beta+\epsilon)^{\top}p^{(h)}\biggr]}_{\displaystyle\textbf{(ii)}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{h^{\prime}=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\mu^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot(X\beta+\epsilon)^{\top}p^{(h)}\cdot(X\beta+\epsilon)^{\top}p^{(h^{\prime})}\biggr]}_{\displaystyle\textbf{(iii)}}+\sigma^{2}.

(C.5)

We let
\epsilon=(\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{L})\in\mathbb{R}^{L}
with
\epsilon_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
be the noise terms in
\{y_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[L]}
.
Notice that
p^{(h)}\in\mathbb{R}^{L}
and
X\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}
.
Recall that
\beta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}/d)
and
x_{q}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
, then we have

\textbf{\small(i)}=\mathbb{E}\left[(\beta^{\top}x_{q})^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[x_{q}^{\top}\beta\beta^{\top}x_{q}\right]=\mathbb{E}\|x_{q}\|^{2}_{2}/d=1.

(C.6)

For the remaining two terms, we can simplify using a similar argument to marginalize
\beta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}/d)
.
Specifically, for term
(ii)
, we have

(ii)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\beta^{\top}x_{q}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\beta^{\top}X^{\top}p^{(h)}\biggr]

\displaystyle=\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[x_{q}^{\top}\beta\beta^{\top}X^{\top}p^{(h)}\right]=\sum_{h=1}^{H}\frac{\mu^{(h)}}{d}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[x_{q}^{\top}X^{\top}p^{(h)}\right].

(C.7)

For term
(iii)
,
using the independence between
\epsilon
and
(X,x_{q},\beta)
, we have

(iii)

\displaystyle=\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{h^{\prime}=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\mu^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[p^{(h^{\prime})^{\top}}(X\beta+\epsilon)(X\beta+\epsilon)^{\top}p^{(h)}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{h^{\prime}=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\mu^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[p^{(h^{\prime})^{\top}}X\beta\beta^{\top}X^{\top}p^{(h)}\right]+\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{h^{\prime}=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\mu^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[p^{(h^{\prime})^{\top}}\epsilon\epsilon^{\top}p^{(h)}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{h^{\prime}=1}^{H}\frac{\mu^{(h)}\mu^{(h^{\prime})}}{d}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[p^{(h^{\prime})^{\top}}XX^{\top}p^{(h)}\right]+\sigma^{2}\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{h^{\prime}=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\mu^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[p^{(h^{\prime})^{\top}}p^{(h)}\right],

(C.8)

where the last equality holds because
\mathbb{E}[\epsilon\epsilon^{\top}]=\sigma^{2}\cdot I_{L}
and
\mathbb{E}[\beta\beta^{\top}]=I_{d}/d
.

In the following, to further simplify the expectation terms in (
C.7
) and (
C.8
), we decouple the randomness of
X\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}
and
x_{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
\{x_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[L]}
and
x_{q}
are i.i.d. random vectors sampled from
\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
. To this end, we take conditional expectations with respect to
X
given
x_{q}
.

Step 2. Take Conditional Expectations given
x_{q}
.

Recall that
p^{(h)}={\mathtt{smax}}(\omega^{(h)}\cdot Xx_{q})
. In the following, for any
\ell\in[L]
, we let
p^{(h)}_{\ell}
and
[{\mathtt{smax}}(\omega^{(h)}\cdot Xx_{q})]_{\ell}
denote the
\ell
-th entry of the attention probability
p^{(h)}
.
Also note that
X=[x_{1},\dots,x_{L}]^{\top}
with
x_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
. For any random vector
x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
and any differentiable function
g
,
Stein’s Lemma states that
\mathbb{E}[xg(x)]=\mathbb{E}[\nabla g(x)]
.
By this lemma, for all
h\in[H]
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[X^{\top}p^{(h)}\mid x_{q}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbb{E}\bigl[x_{\ell}\cdot[{\mathtt{smax}}(\omega^{(h)}\cdot Xx_{q})]_{\ell}{\,|\,}x_{q}\bigr]=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\nabla_{x_{\ell}}~[{\mathtt{smax}}(\omega^{(h)}\cdot Xx_{q})]_{\ell}{\,|\,}x_{q}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\omega^{(h)}\cdot x_{q}\cdot\mathbb{E}\bigl[p^{(h)}_{\ell}-(p^{(h)}_{\ell})^{2}{\,|\,}x_{q}\bigr]=\omega^{(h)}\cdot x_{q}-\omega^{(h)}\cdot x_{q}\cdot\mathbb{E}\bigl[\|p^{(h)}\|_{2}^{2}{\,|\,}x_{q}\bigr].

(C.9)

Here, the second equality follows from Stein’s Lemma, the third equality follows from the derivative of the softmax function, and the last equality follows from the fact that
\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}p^{(h)}_{\ell}=1
.
Combining (
C.9
) and (
C.7
), conditioning on the value of
x_{q}
, we can write the second term in (
C.5
) as

\displaystyle\textbf{\small(ii)}_{{\,|\,}x_{q}}=\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\omega^{(h)}/d\cdot\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}-\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\omega^{(h)}\cdot\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}/d\cdot\mathbb{E}[\|p^{(h)}\|_{2}^{2}\mid x_{q}],

(C.10)

where we use
\textbf{\small(ii)}_{{\,|\,}x_{q}}
to denote the counterpart of
(ii)
with
x_{q}
fixed.
It remains to simplify the third term in (
C.5
).
In particular, we need to handle terms of the form
\mathbb{E}[p^{(h^{\prime})^{\top}}XX^{\top}p^{(h)}\mid x_{q}]
.
To this end, we introduce the following lemma.

Lemma C.2
.

Consider random variable
X=[x_{1},\dots,x_{L}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}
with
x_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
for all
\ell\in[L]
. Let
\omega,\widetilde{\omega}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\upsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be fixed parameters.
We define

p={\mathtt{smax}}(\omega\cdot X\upsilon)
and
\widetilde{p}={\mathtt{smax}}(\widetilde{\omega}\cdot X\upsilon)
.
Then, it holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{p}^{\top}XX^{\top}p]

\displaystyle=d\cdot\mathbb{E}[p^{\top}\widetilde{p}]+\omega\cdot\widetilde{\omega}\cdot\|\upsilon\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}[(1-\|p\|_{2}^{2})\cdot(1-\|\widetilde{p}\|_{2}^{2})]

\displaystyle\quad+2\omega^{2}\cdot\|\upsilon\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}[-\widetilde{p}^{\top}p^{\odot 2}+\widetilde{p}^{\top}p\cdot\|p\|_{2}^{2}]+2\widetilde{\omega}^{2}\cdot\|\upsilon\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}[-p^{\top}\widetilde{p}^{\odot 2}+p^{\top}\widetilde{p}\cdot\|\widetilde{p}\|_{2}^{2}]

\displaystyle\quad+\omega\cdot\widetilde{\omega}\cdot\|\upsilon\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}[p^{\top}\widetilde{p}-p^{\top}\widetilde{p}^{\odot 2}-\widetilde{p}^{\top}p^{\odot 2}+(p^{\top}\widetilde{p})^{2}].

Here
p^{\odot 2}=p\odot p
denotes the element-wise square of
p\in\mathbb{R}^{L}
.

Proof of Lemma
C.2
.

See §
E.1
for a detailed proof.
∎

To compute
\mathbb{E}[p^{(h^{\prime})^{\top}}XX^{\top}p^{(h)}\mid x_{q}]
, we apply Lemma
C.2
with
\omega=\omega^{(h)}
,
\widetilde{\omega}=\omega^{(h^{\prime})}
, and
\upsilon=x_{q}
.
Then, for any
h,h^{\prime}\in[H]
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[p^{(h^{\prime})^{\top}}XX^{\top}p^{(h)}\mid x_{q}]

\displaystyle\quad={\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.0738,0.4711}d\cdot\mathbb{E}[p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}\mid x_{q}]}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5469,0.5469}\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}[({\color[rgb]{0,0.5469,0.5469}1}-\|p^{(h)}\|_{2}^{2})({\color[rgb]{0,0.5469,0.5469}1}-\|p^{(h^{\prime})}\|_{2}^{2})\mid x_{q}]

\displaystyle\qquad+2(\omega^{(h)})^{2}\cdot\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}[-p^{(h^{\prime})^{\top}}p^{(h)^{\odot 2}}+p^{(h^{\prime})^{\top}}p^{(h)}\cdot\|p^{(h)}\|_{2}^{2}\mid x_{q}]

\displaystyle\qquad+2(\omega^{(h^{\prime})})^{2}\cdot\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}[-p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})^{\odot 2}}+p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\|p^{(h^{\prime})}\|_{2}^{2}\mid x_{q}]

\displaystyle\qquad+\omega^{(h)}\cdot\omega^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}[p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}-p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})^{\odot 2}}-p^{(h^{\prime})^{\top}}p^{(h)^{\odot 2}}+(p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})})^{2}\mid x_{q}]

\displaystyle\quad:={\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.0738,0.4711}d\cdot\mathbb{E}[p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}\mid x_{q}]}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5469,0.5469}\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}}+\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot{\mathcal{T}}_{h,h^{\prime}}(x_{q}),

(C.11)

where we use
{\mathcal{T}}_{h,h^{\prime}}(x_{q})
to denote the high-order terms in (
C.11
). Here the high-order terms contain the product of
\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}
and expectations of polynomials of
p^{(h)}
and
p^{(h^{\prime})}
with degrees at least two.

Combine (
C.5
), (
C.6
), (
C.7
) and (
C.8
) and the form of conditional expectations in (
C.9
), (
C.10
), and (
C.11
), we can write the conditional expectation of the loss function given
x_{q}
into a polynomial of
\{p^{(h)}\}_{h\in[H]}
. Specifically, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{|x_{q}}(\omega,\mu):=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(y_{q}-\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}y^{\top}p^{(h)}\bigg)^{2}~\biggl|\,~x_{q}\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad=1+\sigma^{2}-\frac{2\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}}{d}\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\omega^{(h)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}}{d}\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{h^{\prime}=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\mu^{(h^{\prime})}\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})}+(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{h^{\prime}=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\mu^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\mathbb{E}[p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}\mid x_{q}]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{2\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}}{d}\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\omega^{(h)}\cdot\mathbb{E}[\|p^{(h)}\|_{2}^{2}\mid x_{q}]+\frac{\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}}{d}\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{h^{\prime}=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\mu^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot{\mathcal{T}}_{h,h^{\prime}}(x_{q}).

(C.12)

Notice that
\mathcal{L}_{|x_{q}}(\omega,\mu)
above is a function of
x_{q}
. In the following, we analyze the expected value of each term in (
C.12
).

Step 3. Marginalize
x_{q}
and Eliminations of High-order Terms.

Let
\mu=(\mu^{(1)},\dots,\mu^{(H)})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{H}
and
\omega=(\omega^{(1)},\dots,\omega^{(H)})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{H}
.
Since
x_{q}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
, we have
\mathbb{E}[\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}]=d
. By direct calculation, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}}{d}\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\omega^{(h)}\right]=\mu^{\top}\omega,\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}}{d}\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{h^{\prime}=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\mu^{(h^{\prime})}\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})}\right]=(\mu^{\top}\omega)^{2}.

(C.13)

In the following, we show that
\mathbb{E}[p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}]
with large
L
can be approximated by
\exp(d\cdot\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})})/L
(
Step 3.1
), and the remaining terms are mostly high-order and thus can be ignored (
Step 3.2
).

Step 3.1. Approximate
\mathbb{E}[p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}]
under Large
L
.

Next, we approximate
\mathbb{E}[p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}]
by
\exp(d\cdot\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})})/L
under large
L
. This enables us to control the term

(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{h^{\prime}=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\mu^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\mathbb{E}[p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}],

of the loss
L(\omega,\mu)
, as given in (
C.12
).
Our derivation is based on the following insight.
When
\omega^{(h)}
is small, for
p^{(h)}
, we can approximate the denominator in the softmax function by
L
.
Viewing
p^{(h)}
as a function of
\omega^{(h)}
, the first two terms in the Taylor expansion is:

p^{(h)}_{\ell}\approx L^{-1}\cdot(1+\omega^{(h)}\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}),\qquad\forall\ell\in[L].

Here, we approximate the denominator
\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp(\omega^{(h)}\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})
in the softmax function by
L
.
We can similarly write down the Taylor expansion for
p^{(h^{\prime})}
in terms of
\omega^{(h^{\prime})}
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}{\,|\,}x_{q}]

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{L^{2}}\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbb{E}\big[(1+\omega^{(h)}\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})\cdot(1+\omega^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})\big|\,x_{q}\big]

\displaystyle=L^{-1}\cdot(1+\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}).

This implies the following approximation scheme:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}{\,|\,}x_{q}]

\displaystyle\approx L^{-1}\cdot(1+\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot x_{q}^{\top}x_{q})\approx L^{-1}\cdot\exp(\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot x_{q}^{\top}x_{q}).

(C.14)

Similarly, by taking expectation with respect to
x_{q}
in (
C.14
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}]

\displaystyle\approx L^{-1}\cdot\big(1+\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\mathbb{E}[x_{q}^{\top}x_{q}]\big)=L^{-1}\cdot(1+d\cdot\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})})\approx L^{-1}\cdot\exp(d\cdot\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})}).

In the following, we rigorously prove the above approximation.
We prove by leveraging a lemma from
Chen et al. (2024a)
to quantify the accuracy of the approximation in (
C.14
) and the large deviation of
x_{q}
.
We first present the lemma below.

Lemma C.3
.

Let
x_{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be any fixed vector that satisfies
\max\{\|Wx_{q}\|_{2}^{2},\|\widetilde{W}x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}\}\leq\tau\log L
, where
\tau>0
is a constant and
W\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
,
\widetilde{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
are two parameter matrices.
Let
p={\mathtt{smax}}(XWx_{q})
and
\widetilde{p}={\mathtt{smax}}(X\widetilde{W}x_{q})
, where
X=[x_{1},\dots,x_{L}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}
with
x_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
, it holds that

(i).

\left|\mathbb{E}[p^{\top}\widetilde{p}\mid x_{q}]-L^{-1}\cdot\exp\big(x_{q}^{\top}\widetilde{W}^{\top}Wx_{q}\big)\right|=O(L^{-2(1-\epsilon)})
;

(ii).

\max\left\{\mathbb{E}[p^{\top}\widetilde{p}^{\odot 2}\mid x_{q}],~\mathbb{E}[\|p\|_{2}^{2}\cdot p^{\top}\widetilde{p}\mid x_{q}],~\mathbb{E}[(p^{\top}\widetilde{p})^{2}\mid x_{q}]\right\}=O(L^{-2(1-\epsilon)})
,

where we denote
\epsilon=\sqrt{\tau/2}+3/(1+\sqrt{1+1/\tau})
. Furthermore, when
x_{q}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
and
\|\omega\|_{\infty}\lesssim\tau\log L/\max\{d,\log L\}
, we further have

(iii).

\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(\mathbb{E}[p^{\top}\widetilde{p}\mid x_{q}]-L^{-1}\cdot\exp\big(x_{q}^{\top}\widetilde{W}^{\top}Wx_{q}\big)\big)^{2}\Big]=O(L^{-(3-\epsilon)})
.

Note all these three arguments are at population level and thus hold with probability one.

Proof of Lemma
C.3
.

See Lemma B.2 and Lemma B.3 in
Chen et al. (2024a)
for detailed proofs. The first two items are in Lemma B.2, and the third item is in Lemma B.3.
∎

The first item in Lemma
C.3
quantifies the error of the approximation in (
C.14
).
The second item shows that the high-order terms of
p
and
\widetilde{p}
are small.
Additionally, the third item quantifies the squared error of the approximation given
x_{q}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
.
Furthermore, we note that the approximation errors in Lemma
C.3
depends on
\epsilon
, which depends on the parameter
\tau
. To make the approximation errors small, we need to choose a small
\tau
such that the resulting
\epsilon
is small.

To apply this lemma to
p^{(h)}
and
p^{(h^{\prime})}
, we set
W=\omega^{(h)}\cdot I_{d}
and
\widetilde{W}=\omega^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot I_{d}
. To satisfies the requirement that
x_{q}
is bounded in the lemma, we control the tail behavior of
\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}
using the concentration results for
\chi^{2}
-distribution.
By setting
t=\log L
in Lemma
E.1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}>5\max\{d,\log L\}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}>d+2\sqrt{d\log L}+2\log L\right)\leq L^{-1}.

(C.15)

In the following, we assume that
\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{H}
satisfies the following condition:

\textrm{(\sf C1)}\qquad\|\omega\|_{\infty}\leq\kappa\sqrt{\log L/\max\{d,\log L\}},

where
\kappa
denotes a parameter to be determined later.
Based on any
\omega
satisfying (
C1)
, we define
the following good event where
\|\omega^{(h)}x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}
are all bounded:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\sf good}=\big\{\forall h\in[H],~\|\omega^{(h)}\cdot x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 5\kappa^{2}\cdot\log L\big\}.

(C.16)

Then, when
\omega
satisfies (
C1)
, by (
C.15
) we have

\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathcal{E}_{\sf good}^{c}\bigr)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\|\omega\|_{\infty}^{2}\cdot\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}>5\kappa^{2}\log L\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}>5\max\{d,\log L\}\right)\leq L^{-1}.

Besides, when
\mathcal{E}_{\sf good}
holds, the premise of Lemma
C.3
holds with
\tau=5\kappa^{2}
. Now we are ready to work on
\mathbb{E}[p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}]
.
We decompose the
\mathbb{E}[p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}]
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl[p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\ind\bigl(\mathcal{E}_{\sf good}\bigr)\bigr]+\mathbb{E}\bigl[p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\ind\bigl(\mathcal{E}_{\sf good}^{c}\big)\big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\big[p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\ind\big(\mathcal{E}_{\sf good}\big)\big]+L^{-1},

(C.17)

where the last inequality follows from the fact that
p^{(h)^{\top}}
and
p^{(h^{\prime})}
are probability distributions over
[L]
, and thus
p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}\leq 1
. Furthermore, we decompose the first term on the RHS of (
C.17
) as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\ind\big(\mathcal{E}_{\sf good}\big)\big]

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}-L^{-1}\exp(\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}|)\right)\cdot\ind\big(\mathcal{E}_{\sf good}\big)\right]}_{\displaystyle\textbf{(iv)}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\underbrace{L^{-1}\cdot\left(\mathbb{E}\bigl[\exp(\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2})\cdot\ind\big(\mathcal{E}_{\sf good}\big)\big]-\exp(d\cdot\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})})\right)}_{\displaystyle\textbf{(v)}}

\displaystyle\qquad+L^{-1}\cdot\exp(d\cdot\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})})

(C.18)

We control term
(iv)
by applying Lemma
C.3
-(i) with
\tau=5\kappa^{2}
, which implies that there exists and absolute constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\textbf{\small(iv)}\leq\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\big(p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}-L^{-1}\cdot\exp(\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})}\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}|)\big)\cdot\ind\big(\mathcal{E}_{\sf good}\big)~\Big|~x_{q}\right]\right]\right|\leq C_{1}\cdot L^{-2(1-\epsilon)}.

(C.19)

Moreover, here
\epsilon
is a function of specified constant
\kappa
that controls the scaling of
\|\omega\|_{\infty}
, defined as

\displaystyle\epsilon=\kappa\sqrt{5/2}+3/(1+\sqrt{1+(5\kappa^{2})^{-1}}).

(C.20)

To handle term
(v)
, we write it as

(v)

\displaystyle=L^{-1}\cdot\exp(d\cdot\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})})\cdot\Big(\mathbb{E}\big[\exp(\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot(\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}-d))\cdot\ind\big(\mathcal{E}_{\sf good}\big)\big]-1\Big).

Let
\Upsilon
denote the random variable
\exp(\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot(\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}-d))\cdot\ind\big(\mathcal{E}_{\sf good}\big)
, which depends on
x_{q}
only.
Motivated by (
C.15
), we consider the cases where
\ind(\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}-d\leq 2\sqrt{d\log L}+2\log L)
is true or not separately.
Note that when this event is true,
we have

\displaystyle\Upsilon\leq\exp(|\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})}|\cdot(2\sqrt{d\log L}+2\log L)).

(C.21)

When this event does not hold,
we have

\displaystyle\exp(d\cdot\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})})\cdot\Upsilon=\exp(\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2})\cdot\ind\big(\mathcal{E}_{\sf good}\big)\leq\exp(5\kappa^{2}\log L),

(C.22)

thanks to the event
\mathcal{E}_{\sf good}(\omega)
defined in (
C.16
).
Therefore, we conclude that

(v)

\displaystyle=L^{-1}\cdot\exp(d\cdot\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})})\cdot\mathbb{E}[\Upsilon-1]

\displaystyle=L^{-1}\cdot\exp(d\cdot\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})})\cdot\mathbb{E}[\Upsilon\cdot\ind(\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}-d\leq 2\sqrt{d\log L}+2\log L)-1]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+L^{-1}\cdot\exp(d\cdot\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})})\cdot\mathbb{E}[\Upsilon\cdot\ind(\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}-d>2\sqrt{d\log L}+2\log L)]

\displaystyle\leq L^{-1}\cdot\exp(d\cdot\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})})\cdot\left(\exp\bigl(|\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})}|\cdot(2\sqrt{d\log L}+2\log L)\bigr)-1\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+L^{-2}\cdot\exp\left(5\kappa^{2}\cdot\log L\right).

(C.23)

Here, the last inequality follows from (
C.21
) and (
C.22
).
Note that when (
C1)
is true, we have

\displaystyle|\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})}|\cdot(2\sqrt{d\log L}+2\log L)

\displaystyle\leq\kappa^{2}\cdot\log L/\max\{d,\log L\}\cdot(2\sqrt{d\log L}+2\log L)\leq 4\kappa^{2}\cdot\log L,

\displaystyle\exp(d\cdot\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})})

\displaystyle\leq\exp\bigl(\kappa^{2}\cdot\log L/\max\{d,\log L\}\cdot d\bigr)\leq L^{\kappa^{2}}.

Thus, in this case, we have
By substituting the arguments above back into (
C.23
), we obtain that

(v)

\displaystyle\leq L^{-1+5\kappa^{2}}+L^{-2+5\kappa^{2}}\leq 2L^{-1+5\kappa^{2}}.

(C.24)

Combining (
C.17
), (
C.18
), (
C.19
) and (
C.24
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}]

\displaystyle=L^{-1}\cdot\exp(d\cdot\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})})+C_{1}\cdot L^{-2(1-\epsilon)}+2L^{-1+5\kappa^{2}}+L^{-1}

\displaystyle=L^{-1}\cdot\exp(d\cdot\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})})+C_{1}\cdot L^{-2(1-\epsilon)}+3L^{-1+5\kappa^{2}},

where we define
\epsilon
as
\epsilon=\kappa\sqrt{5/2}+3/(1+\sqrt{1+1/(5\kappa^{2})})
, which is a function of the specified constant
\kappa
, and
C_{1}
is an absolute constant coming from (
C.19
).
Thus, we conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{h^{\prime}=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\mu^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\mathbb{E}[p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}]

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{1}{L}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{h^{\prime}=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\mu^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\exp(d\cdot\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})})+\|\mu\|_{\infty}^{2}\cdot H^{2}\cdot\bigl(C_{1}\cdot L^{-2(1-\epsilon)}+3L^{-1+5\kappa^{2}}\bigr).

(C.25)

This enables us to bound the corresponding term in the loss function
L(\omega,\mu)
, as given in (
C.12
).

Step 3.2. Bound
\mathbb{E}\left[\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}[\|p^{(h)}\|_{2}^{2}\mid x_{q}]\right]
under Large
L
.

Next, we bound the term

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{2\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}}{d}\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\omega^{(h)}\cdot\mathbb{E}[\|p^{(h)}\|_{2}^{2}\mid x_{q}]\bigg],

in the decomposition of the loss
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\omega,\mu)
.
Recall that
\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}\sim\chi^{2}_{d}
. Thus, using the second moment of
\chi^{2}_{d}
, we have
\mathbb{E}\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{4}=d\cdot(d+2)
.
Now, applying the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\omega^{(h)}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}[\|p^{(h)}\|_{2}^{2}\mid x_{q}]\right]\leq\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\omega^{(h)}\cdot\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\bigl[\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{4}\bigl]\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\bigl(\mathbb{E}[\|p^{(h)}\|_{2}^{2}\mid x_{q}]\bigr)^{2}\big]}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim d\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\omega^{(h)}\cdot\biggl(\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\big[\left(\mathbb{E}[\|p^{(h)}\|_{2}^{2}\mid x_{q}]-L^{-1}\cdot\exp\bigl(d\omega^{(h),2}\bigr)\right)^{2}\big]}+L^{-1}\cdot\exp\bigl(d\omega^{(h),2}\bigr)\biggl).

Here, in the last inequality, we use
\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]}\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[(X-a)^{2}]}+|a|
, where
X
is any random variable and
a
is a constant. Recall that we use
a\lesssim b
to denote that
a\leq C\cdot b
for some absolute constant
C>0
.
By applying Lemma
C.3
-(iii) with
\tau=5\kappa^{2}
, we obtain the following inequality:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\big(\mathbb{E}[\|p^{(h)}\|_{2}^{2}\mid x_{q}]-L^{-1}\cdot\exp\bigl(d\omega^{(h),2}\bigr)\big)^{2}\bigr]\leq C_{2}\cdot L^{-3(1-\epsilon)},

where
C_{2}
is an absolute constant and
\epsilon
is defined in (
C.20
).
In addition, under the scaling condition
(C1)
, for all
h\in[H]
, we have
\exp(d\omega^{(h)^{2}})\leq L^{\kappa^{2}}
.
Therefore, we conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\omega^{(h)}\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}[\|p^{(h)}\|_{2}^{2}\mid x_{q}]\big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\mu\|_{\infty}\cdot\|\omega\|_{\infty}\cdot dH\cdot\bigl(L^{-(3-\epsilon)/2}+L^{-1+\kappa^{2}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\|\mu\|_{\infty}\cdot dH\cdot\bigl(L^{-(3-\epsilon)/2}+L^{-1+\kappa^{2}}\bigr),

(C.26)

where the last inequality follows from the fact that
\kappa\leq 1
.

Step 3.3. Bound High-order Terms
\mathbb{E}[\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot{\mathcal{T}}_{h,h^{\prime}}(x_{q})]
under large
L
.

Recall that we define high-order terms
{\mathcal{T}}_{h,h^{\prime}}(x_{q})
as in (
C.11
), which comes from the decomposition of
\mathbb{E}[p^{(h^{\prime})^{\top}}XX^{\top}p^{(h)}\mid x_{q}]
.
As shown in (
C.11
),
{\mathcal{T}}_{h,h^{\prime}}(x_{q})
is composed of terms such as

\mathbb{E}[p^{\top}\widetilde{p}^{\odot 2}\mid x_{q}],\qquad\mathbb{E}[\|p\|_{2}^{2}\cdot p^{\top}\widetilde{p}\mid x_{q}],\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\mathbb{E}[(p^{\top}\widetilde{p})^{2}\mid x_{q}],

where we denote
\{p,\widetilde{p}\}=\{p^{(h)},p^{(h^{\prime})}\}
.
Also notice that, by (
C.11
), each term in
{\mathcal{T}}_{h,h^{\prime}}(x_{q})
involves has multiplicative factors
(\omega^{(h)})^{2}
, or
(\omega^{(h^{\prime})})^{2}
, or
\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})}
.
Following a similar argument as in
Step 3.2
, we apply Cauchy-Schwartz inequality to bound the high-order terms as

\displaystyle\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{h^{\prime}=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\mu^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\mathbb{E}[\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot{\mathcal{T}}_{h,h^{\prime}}(x_{q})]\lesssim d\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\mu^{(h^{\prime})}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\big[\bigl([\mathbb{E}[{\mathcal{T}}_{h,h^{\prime}}(x_{q})\mid x_{q}]\bigr)^{2}\big]}.

(C.27)

Then we apply Lemma
C.3
-(ii) with
\tau=5\kappa^{2}
to the terms in (
C.27
), which implies that

[\mathbb{E}[{\mathcal{T}}_{h,h^{\prime}}(x_{q})\mid x_{q}]\leq C_{3}\cdot\|\omega\|_{\infty}^{2}\cdot L^{-2(1-\epsilon)}\lesssim L^{-2(1-\epsilon)},

where
\epsilon
is defined in (
C.20
). By (
C.27
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{h^{\prime}=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\mu^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\mathbb{E}[\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot{\mathcal{T}}_{h,h^{\prime}}(x_{q})]\lesssim\|\mu\|_{\infty}^{2}\cdot dH^{2}\cdot L^{-2(1-\epsilon)}.

(C.28)

This inequality establishes an upper bound on the last term in (
C.12
).

Step 4. Combine Everything and Conclude the Proof.

Now we take the expectation with respect to
x_{q}
in (
C.12
) and get

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\omega,\mu)

\displaystyle=1+\sigma^{2}-\underbrace{\frac{2\mathbb{E}[\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}]}{d}\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\omega^{(h)}}_{\displaystyle\eqref{eq:loss_exp_23}}+\underbrace{\frac{\mathbb{E}[\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}]}{d}\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{h^{\prime}=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\mu^{(h^{\prime})}\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})}}_{\displaystyle\eqref{eq:loss_exp_23}}

\displaystyle\qquad+(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot\underbrace{\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{h^{\prime}=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\mu^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\mathbb{E}[p^{(h)^{\top}}p^{(h^{\prime})}]}_{\displaystyle\eqref{eq:loss_exp_4}}+\underbrace{\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\omega^{(h)}\cdot\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{2\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}}{d}\cdot\mathbb{E}[\|p^{(h)}\|_{2}^{2}\mid x_{q}]\bigg]}_{\displaystyle\eqref{eq:loss_exp_5}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\underbrace{\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{h^{\prime}=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\mu^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{\|x_{q}\|_{2}^{2}}{d}\cdot{\mathcal{T}}_{h,h^{\prime}}(x_{q})\biggr]}_{{}_{\displaystyle\eqref{eq:loss_exp_6}}}.

Here we list the number of inequalities that bound each term.
Combining (
C.13
), (
C.25
), (
C.26
) and (
C.28
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\omega,\mu)

\displaystyle=1+\sigma^{2}-2\mu^{\top}\omega+(\mu^{\top}\omega^{2})+\frac{1}{L}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{h^{\prime}=1}^{H}\mu^{(h)}\mu^{(h^{\prime})}\cdot\exp(d\cdot\omega^{(h)}\omega^{(h^{\prime})})+\mathsf{Err}_{\kappa}

\displaystyle=1+\sigma^{2}-2\mu^{\top}\omega+\mu^{\top}\Big(\omega\omega^{\top}+(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot L^{-1}\cdot\exp(d\omega\omega^{\top})\Big)\mu+\mathsf{Err}_{\kappa},

where we error term
\mathsf{Err}_{\kappa}
is bounded via

\displaystyle\mathsf{Err}_{\kappa}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mu\|_{\infty}^{2}\cdot H^{2}\cdot\bigl(L^{-2(1-\epsilon)}+L^{-1+5\kappa^{2}}\bigr)+\|\mu\|_{\infty}\cdot dH\cdot\bigl(L^{-(3-\epsilon)/2}+L^{-1+\kappa^{2}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\|\mu\|_{\infty}^{2}\cdot dH^{2}\cdot L^{-2(1-\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\max\{\|\mu\|_{\infty},\|\mu\|_{\infty}^{2}\}\cdot dH^{2}\cdot\max\bigl\{L^{-2(1-\epsilon)},L^{-(3-\epsilon)/2},L^{-(1-5\kappa^{2})}\bigr\}.

(C.29)

Here
\epsilon
is defined in (
C.20
) and
\kappa
is a small constant.
By taking
\kappa=0.1
such that
\epsilon\approx 0.69\leq 0.7
, the approximation error can be further simplified as
\mathsf{Err}_{\kappa}\lesssim\max\{\|\mu\|_{\infty},\|\mu\|_{\infty}^{2}\}\cdot dH^{2}\cdot L^{-2(1-\epsilon)}\leq\max\{\|\mu\|_{\infty},\|\mu\|_{\infty}^{2}\}\cdot dH^{2}\cdot L^{-3/5}
.
Hence, for a given error level
\lambda>0
, if we assume
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{H}
satisfies

\textrm{(\sf C2)}\qquad\max\{\|\mu\|_{\infty},\|\mu\|_{\infty}^{2}\}\lesssim L^{-\lambda/2+3/10},

then we can control the error by
\mathsf{Err}_{\kappa}=O(dH^{2}\cdot L^{-\lambda})
. This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix D
Proof of Theorem
4.2

In this section, we prove Theorem
4.2
, the main result of this paper.
We present separate proofs for each part of the theorem in the following subsections.
Recall that we use
\theta=(\omega,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{2H}
to parameterize the multi-head attention model with
H
heads, where the weight matrices satisfy (
3.1
).
Furthermore, recall that we define the parameter spaces
\mathscr{S}^{*}
and

\bar{\mathscr{S}}
in (
4.7
) and (
4.13
), respectively, with
\mathscr{S}^{*}\subseteq\bar{\mathscr{S}}
.
We define
\sum_{h\in\mathcal{H}_{+}}\mu^{(h)}=\mu_{+}
and
\sum_{h\in\mathcal{H}_{-}}\mu^{(h)}=\mu_{-}
to simplify the notation.
Also recall that we define the debiased GD predictor with learning
\eta
as

\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf gd}(\eta)=\frac{\eta}{L}\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}y_{\ell}\cdot\bar{x}_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q},\quad\text{with}\quad\bar{x}_{\ell}=x_{\ell}-\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}x_{\ell}.

(D.1)

Note that here we use
\bar{x}_{\ell}
to denote the centered covariate.

In the following, we present a proof of Theorem
4.2
.
In §
D.1
, we show that the multi-head attention model can approximate the debiased GD predictor with a small error.
In §
D.2
, we show that minimizing the approximate loss in (
4.1
) over the parameter space
\bar{\mathscr{S}}
leads to a multi-head attention model that converges to the debiased GD predictor. In §
D.3
, we show that the debiased GD predictor is asymptotically Bayes optimal up to a proportionality factor.

D.1
Proof of Theorem
4.2
-(i): Approximation

Proof of Theorem
4.2
-(i).

In this proof, we regard the learning rate
\eta
as a constant.
Let
\gamma>0
be a scaling parameter and let
\breve{\mu}
be defined such that
2\breve{\mu}\gamma=\eta
.
Since we assumed that
\mu_{+}=-\mu_{-}=\breve{\mu}
with
\breve{\mu}>0
and
(\omega,\mu)\in\bar{\mathscr{S}}
, the multi-head attention estimator takes the form

\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{q}(\theta)=\breve{\mu}\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\frac{y_{\ell}\cdot\exp(\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp(\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})}-\breve{\mu}\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\frac{y_{\ell}\cdot\exp(-\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp(-\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})},

(D.2)

and the debiased GD predictor is defined in (
D.1
) with
\eta=2\gamma\breve{\mu}
.
Following this, we the decompose difference between transformer
\widehat{y}_{q}(\theta)
and debiased GD
\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf gd}(\eta)
as

\displaystyle\breve{\mu}^{-1}\cdot\{\widehat{y}_{q}(\theta)-\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf gd}(\eta)\}

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{L}\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\left(\exp(\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})-\exp(-\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})-2\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}\right)\cdot y_{\ell}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{L-\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp(\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})}{L\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp(\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})}\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp(\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})\cdot y_{\ell}+\frac{\gamma}{L}\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\bar{x}^{\top}x_{q}\cdot y_{\ell}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp(-\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})-L}{L\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp(-\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})}\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp(-\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})\cdot y_{\ell}+\frac{\gamma}{L}\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\bar{x}^{\top}x_{q}\cdot y_{\ell}

\displaystyle\qquad=\textbf{\small(i)}+\textbf{\small(ii)}+\textbf{\small(iii)}.

(D.3)

Here we define the three terms as
(i)
,
(ii)
and
(iii)
to simplify the notation.
Before delving into details, we first define three auxiliary functions over
(\gamma,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
as below:

\displaystyle\phi_{1}(\gamma,x)=

\displaystyle\exp(\gamma x)-\exp(-\gamma x)-2\gamma x,~~\phi_{2}(\gamma,x)=\exp(\gamma x)-1-\gamma x,~~\psi(\gamma,x)=\exp(\gamma x)-1.

By Taylor expansion, we can obtain that
\phi_{1}(\gamma,x)
and
\phi_{2}(\gamma,x)
are both
O(\gamma^{2}x^{2})
when
\gamma\cdot x
is small, and
\psi(\gamma,x)=O(\gamma x)
.
We use
\phi_{1}
to bound term
(i)
in (
D.3
) as follows:

\displaystyle\bigl|\textbf{\small(i)}\bigr|=\bigg|\frac{1}{L}\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\phi_{1}(\gamma,x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})\cdot y_{\ell}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\ell\in[L]}|\phi_{1}(\gamma,x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})|\cdot\frac{\|y\|_{2}}{\sqrt{L}}=\phi_{1}\Big(\gamma,\max_{\ell\in{[L]}}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|\Big)\cdot\frac{\|y\|_{2}}{\sqrt{L}}.

(D.4)

Here the inequality results from the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality and the last equality follows from the fact that
\phi_{1}(\gamma,\cdot)
is an even function and monotonically increasing for
\gamma>0
.
Next we consider the term
(ii)
.
To simplify the notation, we define
{\mathcal{S}}_{\sf exp}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp(\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})
and then we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}-\phi_{2}(\gamma,x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\Bigl(1+\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}-\exp(\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})\Bigr)=L+\gamma\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}-{\mathcal{S}}_{\sf exp}.

(D.5)

Based on the definition of term
(ii)
in (
D.3
), we have

(ii)

\displaystyle=\frac{L-{\mathcal{S}}_{\sf exp}}{L\cdot{\mathcal{S}}_{\sf exp}}\cdot\biggl(\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp(\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})\cdot y_{\ell}\biggr)+\frac{\gamma}{L}\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\bar{x}^{\top}x_{q}\cdot y_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L\cdot{\mathcal{S}}_{\sf exp}}\cdot\biggl(\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}-\phi_{2}(\gamma,x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})-\gamma\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}\biggr)\cdot\biggl(\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp(\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})\cdot y_{\ell}\biggr)+\frac{\gamma}{L}\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\bar{x}^{\top}x_{q}\cdot y_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L\cdot{\mathcal{S}}_{\sf exp}}\cdot\biggl(\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}-\phi_{2}(\gamma,x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})\biggr)\cdot\biggl(\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp(\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})\cdot y_{\ell}\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{\gamma}{L\cdot{\mathcal{S}}_{\sf exp}}\cdot\biggl(\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}\biggr)\cdot\biggl(\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp(\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})\cdot y_{\ell}\biggr)+\frac{\gamma}{L}\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\bar{x}^{\top}x_{q}\cdot y_{\ell}.

(D.6)

Here, the first equality follows from (
D.5
). To simplify the notation, we define

(ii.a)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}-\phi_{2}(\gamma,x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})\bigg)\cdot\bigg(\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp(\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})\cdot y_{\ell}\bigg),\quad\textbf{\small(ii.b)}=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\psi(\gamma,x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})\cdot y_{\ell}.

Then, the first term in (
D.6
) corresponds to
L/{\mathcal{S}}_{\sf exp}\cdot\textbf{\small(ii.a)}
. Furthermore, to handle the last two terms in (
D.6
), we denote
\bar{x}=1/L\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}x_{\ell}
and
\bar{y}=1/L\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}y_{\ell}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma}{L\cdot{\mathcal{S}}_{\sf exp}}\cdot\biggl(\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}\biggr)\cdot\biggl(\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp(\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})\cdot y_{\ell}\biggr)-\frac{\gamma}{L}\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\bar{x}^{\top}x_{q}\cdot y_{\ell}

\displaystyle\qquad=\gamma\cdot\bar{x}^{\top}x_{q}\cdot\bigg(L/{\mathcal{S}}_{\sf exp}\cdot\textbf{\small(ii.b)}+(1-L/{\mathcal{S}}_{\sf exp})\cdot\bar{y}\bigg),

(D.7)

Combining (
D.6
) and (
D.7
), we can upper bound term
(ii)
as follows:

\displaystyle\big|\textbf{\small(ii)}\big|\leq L/{\mathcal{S}}_{\sf exp}\cdot\bigr(|\textbf{\small(ii.a)}|+\gamma\cdot|\bar{x}^{\top}x_{q}|\cdot\big|\textbf{\small(ii.b)}\big|+\gamma\cdot|\bar{x}^{\top}x_{q}|\cdot({\mathcal{S}}_{\sf exp}/L-1)\cdot|\bar{y}|\bigr).

Furthermore, we can easily see that
\exp(\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})\geq\ind(x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}>0)
for all
\ell
,
\bar{x}^{\top}x_{q}\leq\max_{\ell\in[L]}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|
and
|\bar{y}|\leq\|y\|_{2}/\sqrt{L}
.
Thus, we can upper bound
(ii)
as

\displaystyle\big|\textbf{\small(ii)}\big|\leq\biggl(\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\ind(x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}>0)\biggr)^{-1}\cdot\Bigl(|\textbf{\small(ii.a)}|+\gamma\cdot\max_{\ell\in[L]}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|\cdot\Big\{\big|\textbf{\small(ii.b)}\big|+\big|\textbf{\small(ii.c)}\big|\cdot\frac{\|y\|_{2}}{\sqrt{L}}\Big\}\Bigr).

(D.8)

We first consider
(ii.a)
. Note the
\phi_{2}
satisfies
|\phi_{2}(\gamma,x)|\leq\phi_{2}(\gamma,|x|)
, and for any fixed
\gamma>0
,
\phi_{2}(\gamma,\cdot)
is monotonically increasing in
x
on
[0,\infty)
.
Following a similar argument as in (
D.4
), we have

\displaystyle\bigl|\textbf{\small(ii.a)}\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\phi_{2}\Big(\gamma,\max_{\ell\in{[L]}}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(\gamma\cdot\max_{\ell\in{[L]}}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|\Big)\cdot\frac{\|y\|_{2}}{\sqrt{L}}.

(D.9)

Similarly, for term
(ii.b)
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{\sf exp}/L-1
, using the facts that
|\psi(\gamma,\cdot)|\leq\psi(\gamma,|\cdot|)
and
\psi(\gamma,\cdot)>0
monotone increasing on
[0,\infty)
for
\gamma>0
, we have

\displaystyle\bigl|\textbf{\small(ii.b)}\bigr|\leq\psi\Big(\gamma,\max_{\ell\in{[L]}}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|\Big)\cdot\frac{\|y\|_{2}}{\sqrt{L}},\quad\bigl|{\mathcal{S}}_{\sf exp}/L-1\bigr|=1/L\cdot|{\mathcal{S}}_{\sf exp}-L|\leq\psi\Big(\gamma,\max_{\ell\in{[L]}}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|\Big).

(D.10)

Therefore, combining (
D.8
), (
D.9
) and (
D.10
), we obtain

\displaystyle\bigl|\textbf{\small(ii)}\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\biggl(\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\ind(x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}>0)\biggr)^{-1}\cdot\biggl\{\phi_{2}\Big(\gamma,\max_{\ell\in{[L]}}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(\gamma\cdot\max_{\ell\in{[L]}}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|\Big)\cdot\frac{\|y\|_{2}}{\sqrt{L}}

\displaystyle\qquad+2\gamma\cdot\psi\Big(\gamma,\max_{\ell\in{[L]}}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|\Big)\cdot\max_{\ell\in[L]}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|\cdot\frac{\|y\|_{2}}{\sqrt{L}}\biggl\}.

(D.11)

For term
(iii)
in (
D.3
), by symmetry, we can derive a similar bound as in (
D.11
) by changing the parameter
\gamma
to
-\gamma
. Note that
|\phi_{2}(-\gamma,\cdot)|\leq\phi_{2}(\gamma,|\cdot|)
and
|\psi(-\gamma,\cdot)|\leq\psi(\gamma,|\cdot|)
given
\gamma>0
. In addition,

\exp(-\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})\geq\ind(x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}<0)
for all
\ell\in[L]
.
Thus, similar to (
D.11
), we have

(iii)

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\ind(x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}\leq 0)\bigg)^{-1}\cdot\biggl\{\phi_{2}\Big(\gamma,\max_{\ell\in{[L]}}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(\gamma\cdot\max_{\ell\in{[L]}}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|\Big)\cdot\frac{\|y\|_{2}}{\sqrt{L}}

\displaystyle\qquad+2\gamma\cdot\psi\Big(\gamma,\max_{\ell\in{[L]}}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|\Big)\cdot\max_{\ell\in[L]}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|\cdot\frac{\|y\|_{2}}{\sqrt{L}}\biggl\},

(D.12)

Combining (
D.3
), (
D.4
), (
D.11
) and (
D.12
), we establish an upper bound on
\widehat{y}_{q}(\theta)-\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf gd}(\eta)
using
\breve{\mu}\asymp\gamma^{-1}
.
To simplify the expression, we apply the concentration concentration results, stated in Lemma
E.3
. With probability at least
1-\delta
, these three inequalities hold simultaneously:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \max_{\ell\in[L]}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|&\leq\sqrt{12d}\cdot\log(8L/\delta),\quad\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}y_{\ell}^{2}\leq 1+\sigma^{2}+{\rm poly}(d^{-1/2},L^{-1/2},\log(1/\delta)),\\
&\qquad\left|\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\ind(x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}>0)-\frac{1}{2}\right|\leq\min\big\{\sqrt{\log(8/\delta)/L},1/2\big\}.\end{aligned}

(D.13)

When
L\geq 16\cdot\log(8/\delta)
, we have
1/L\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\ind(x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}\leq 0)\in[0.25,0.75],
with high probability. When this is the case,
1/L\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\ind(x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}>0)
also lies in
[0.25,0.75]
.
Following (
D.13
) and the upper bound on
(i)
in (
D.4
), we have

\displaystyle\gamma^{-1}\cdot\bigl|\textbf{\small(i)}\bigr|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{1+\sigma^{2}}\cdot\gamma^{-1}\cdot\phi_{1}\Bigl(\gamma,\max_{\ell\in[L]}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|\Bigr)

\displaystyle\asymp\sqrt{1+\sigma^{2}}\cdot\gamma\cdot\max_{\ell\in[L]}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|^{2}=O\bigl(\sqrt{1+\sigma^{2}}\cdot\gamma\cdot d\cdot\log^{2}(8L/\delta)\bigr),

(D.14)

where
O(\cdot)
hides constant terms that are independent of
d
,
L
, and
\delta
.
Now, we consider the case where
\gamma
is a sufficiently small constant such that
\gamma\leq C_{1}\cdot(\sqrt{d}\cdot\log(L/\delta))^{-1}
and thus
\exp\big(\gamma\cdot\max_{\ell\in[L]}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|\big)\lesssim 1
.
Similarly, using the upper bound on
(ii)
given in (
D.11
), we have

\displaystyle\gamma^{-1}\cdot\bigl|\textbf{\small(ii)}\bigr|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{1+\sigma^{2}}\cdot\gamma^{-1}\cdot\phi_{2}\Bigl(\gamma,\max_{\ell\in[L]}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|\Bigr)\cdot\exp\Bigl(\gamma\cdot\max_{\ell\in[L]}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sqrt{1+\sigma^{2}}\cdot 2\psi\Big(\gamma,\max_{\ell\in{[L]}}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|\Big)\cdot\max_{\ell\in[L]}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|

\displaystyle\asymp\sqrt{1+\sigma^{2}}\cdot\gamma\cdot\max_{\ell\in[L]}|x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}|^{2}=O\bigl(\sqrt{1+\sigma^{2}}\cdot\gamma\cdot d\cdot\log^{2}(8L/\delta)\bigr).

(D.15)

We can also get the same upper bound for
(iii)
, using (
D.12
).
Recall that we let
\theta=(\omega,\mu)
be the transformer parameter with

\omega=(\gamma,-\gamma)
and
\mu=(\breve{\mu},-\breve{\mu})
, where
\gamma,\breve{\mu}>0
with
\eta=2\gamma\cdot\breve{\mu}
, which is a constant.
Combining (
D.3
), (
D.14
) and (
D.15
), we conclude that

\displaystyle|\widehat{y}_{q}(\theta)-\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf gd}(\eta)|\lesssim\gamma^{-1}\cdot\big(\bigl|\textbf{\small(i)}\bigr|+\bigl|\textbf{\small(ii)}\bigr|+\bigl|\textbf{\small(iii)}\bigr|\big)=\widetilde{O}\bigl(\sqrt{1+\sigma^{2}}\cdot\gamma\cdot d\bigr),

(D.16)

where
\widetilde{O}(\cdot)
omits logarithmic terms.
Therefore, we complete the proof.
∎

Remark D.1
.

Here, we also provide a heuristic but simpler derivation of the debiased GD predictor using first-order Taylor expansion expansion. Note that

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\frac{y_{\ell}\cdot\exp(\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp(\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})}

\displaystyle\approx\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\frac{y_{\ell}\cdot(1+\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})}{L+\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}}\approx\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\frac{y_{\ell}}{L}\cdot\left(1+\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}-\frac{\gamma}{L}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}\right),

where the second approximation results from the first-order approximation of reciprocal and ignoring the higher-order
O(\gamma^{2})
terms. Similarly, we can show that

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\frac{y_{\ell}\cdot\exp(-\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp(-\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})}\approx\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\frac{y_{\ell}}{L}\cdot\left(1-\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}+\frac{\gamma}{L}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q}\right).

By subtracting the two terms above, we can get the desired form of approximation, i.e.,
\widehat{y}_{q}(\theta)\approx\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf gd}(\eta)
,
where
\eta=2\breve{\mu}\gamma
and
\widehat{y}_{q}(\theta)
is given in (
D.2
).

D.2
Proof of Theorem
4.2
-(ii): Optimality

Proof of Theorem
4.2
-(ii).

Recall that for fixed
\gamma>0
, we consider the parameter space defined as

\displaystyle\bar{\mathscr{S}}=

\displaystyle\{(\omega,\mu):~\forall\gamma>0,~\omega^{(h)}=\gamma\cdot{\rm sign}(\omega^{(h)})\mathrm{~for~all~}h\in[H],~\min\{|\mathcal{H}_{+}|,|\mathcal{H}_{-}|\}>1\}.

For notational simplicity, we denote

\mu_{+}=\sum_{h\in\mathcal{H}^{+}}\mu^{(h)}
,
\mu_{-}=\sum_{h\in\mathcal{H}^{-}}\mu^{(h)}
and
\mu_{d}=\sum_{h\in[H]\backslash\mathcal{H}_{+}\cup\mathcal{H}_{-}}\mu^{(h)}
, respectively as the sum of OV parameters for the positive, negative and dummy heads.
With this parameterization, the transformer estimator takes the form

\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{q}(\theta)=\mu_{+}\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\frac{y_{\ell}\cdot\exp(\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp(\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})}+\mu_{-}\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\frac{y_{\ell}\cdot\exp(-\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp(-\gamma\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q})}+\mu_{d}\cdot\bar{y},

and thus it suffices to
consider three-head attention with
\omega=(\gamma,-\gamma,0)
and
\mu=(\mu_{+},\mu_{-},\mu_{d})
.
Recall that we introduce the approximate loss
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\omega,\mu)
in (
4.1
), which takes the following form:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\omega,\mu)

\displaystyle=1-2\mu^{\top}\omega+\mu^{\top}\big(\omega\omega^{\top}+(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot L^{-1}\cdot\exp(d\omega\omega^{\top})\big)\mu

\displaystyle=(1-\mu^{\top}\omega)^{2}+(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot L^{-1}\cdot\mu^{\top}\exp(d\omega\omega^{\top})\mu.

Consider the problem of minimizing this loss function
\min_{\omega,\mu}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\omega,\mu)
.
For notational simplicity, we let
\omega_{a}=(\gamma,-\gamma)
and
\mu_{a}=(\mu_{+},\mu_{-})
denote the parameters of active heads and let
\mu_{d}
denote the OV parameters of dummy heads with
\omega_{d}=0
. Then, we can write the loss as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\omega_{a},\mu_{a},\mu_{d})

\displaystyle=(1-\mu_{a}^{\top}\omega_{a})^{2}+(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot L^{-1}\cdot\bigl(\mu_{a}^{\top}\exp(d\omega_{a}\omega_{a}^{\top})\mu_{a}+\mu_{d}^{2}+2\mu_{d}\cdot\mu_{a}^{\top}{\bm{1}}\bigr),

(D.17)

where the last term
2\mu_{d}\cdot\mu_{a}^{\top}{\bm{1}}
is due to the interaction between the OV of dummy heads and the OV of active heads.
In the following, we find the minimizer of
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\omega_{a},\mu_{a},\mu_{d})
.
We first optimize
\mu_{d}\in\mathbb{R}

with
\omega_{a}
and
\mu_{a}
fixed.
Note that
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
is quadratic with respect to
\mu_{d}
. By direct calculation, we obtain that the optimal value of
\mu_{d}
is
\mu_{d}^{*}=-\mu_{a}^{\top}{\bm{1}}
.
Plugging
\mu_{d}^{*}
in to the loss
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
in (
D.17
), we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\omega_{a},\mu_{a},\mu_{d}^{*})

\displaystyle=(1-\mu_{a}^{\top}\omega_{a})^{2}+(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot L^{-1}\cdot\bigl(\mu_{a}^{\top}\exp(d\omega_{a}\omega_{a}^{\top})\mu_{a}-(\mu_{a}^{\top}{\bm{1}})^{2}\bigr).

(D.18)

With slight abuse of notation, we write
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\omega_{a},\mu_{a},\mu_{d}^{*})
as
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\omega_{a},\mu_{a})
in the sequel.
By rearranging the terms, we can rewrite the above loss as a function of
\omega_{a}
and
\mu_{a}
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\omega_{a},\mu_{a})=1-2\omega_{a}^{\top}\mu_{a}+\mu_{a}^{\top}\bigl(\omega_{a}\omega_{a}^{\top}+(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot L^{-1}\cdot\bigl(\exp\bigl(d\,\omega_{a}\omega_{a}^{\top}\bigr)-\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\bigr)\bigr)\mu_{a}.

(D.19)

We note that the two-dimensional vector
\omega_{a}
is orthogonal to
{\bm{1}}=(1,1)
. Moreover,
\omega_{a}
and
{\bm{1}}
form an unnormalized orthogonal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
To simplify the notation, we define
A(\omega_{a})=\omega_{a}\omega_{a}^{\top}+(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot L^{-1}\cdot(\exp(d\omega_{a}\omega_{a}^{\top})-{\bm{1}}{\bm{1}}^{\top})
.
Let us decompose
\exp(d\omega_{a}\omega_{a}^{\top})
into

\displaystyle\exp(d\omega_{a}\omega_{a}^{\top})

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp(d\gamma^{2})+\exp(-d\gamma^{2})}{2}\cdot{\bm{1}}{\bm{1}}^{\top}+\frac{\exp(d\gamma^{2})-\exp(-d\gamma^{2})}{2\gamma^{2}}\cdot\omega_{a}\omega_{a}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\cosh(d\gamma^{2})\cdot{\bm{1}}{\bm{1}}^{\top}+\sinh(d\gamma^{2})\cdot\frac{\omega_{a}\omega_{a}^{\top}}{\gamma^{2}}.

Thus,
A(\omega_{a})
allows the following rank-one decomposition:

\displaystyle A(\omega_{a})

\displaystyle=\omega_{a}\omega_{a}^{\top}+(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot L^{-1}\cdot\bigl(\cosh(d\gamma^{2})\cdot{\bm{1}}{\bm{1}}^{\top}+\sinh(d\gamma^{2})\cdot\frac{\omega_{a}\omega_{a}^{\top}}{\gamma^{2}}-{\bm{1}}{\bm{1}}^{\top}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\Big(1+(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot L^{-1}\cdot\sinh(d\gamma^{2})\cdot\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\Big)\cdot\omega_{a}\omega_{a}^{\top}+(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot L^{-1}\cdot\bigl(\cosh(d\gamma^{2})-1\bigr)\cdot{\bm{1}}{\bm{1}}^{\top}.

In other words,
\omega_{a}
and
{\bm{1}}
are just the (unnormalized) eigenvectors of
A(\omega_{a})
.
In particular, since
\sinh(x)\geq 0
and
\cosh(x)-1\geq 0
for all
x\geq 0
, the matrix
A(\omega_{a})
is positive semi-definite.
Thus, minimizing the objective in (
D.19
) is just a convex optimization problem.
Optimizing this quadratic function over
\mu_{a}
with
\omega_{a}
fixed, the optimal value of
\mu_{a}
,
denoted by
\mu_{a}^{*}(\omega_{a})
, is given by

\displaystyle\mu_{a}^{*}(\omega_{a})

\displaystyle=A(\omega_{a})^{-1}\omega_{a}.

(D.20)

Plugging
\mu_{a}^{*}(\omega_{a})
in (
D.20
) into
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
,
we obtain the
a closed-form expression of the optimal loss with respect to
\omega_{a}
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\omega_{a})=\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}\bigl(\omega_{a},\mu_{a}^{*}(\omega_{a}),\mu_{d}^{*}\bigr)=1-\omega_{a}^{\top}A(\omega_{a})^{-1}\,\omega_{a}.

(D.21)

Here we abuse the notation slightly by writing the loss function as a function of
\omega_{a}
only.

From the discussion above, we see that the inverse of
A(\omega_{a})
is given by

\displaystyle A(\omega_{a})^{-1}=\Big(1+(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot L^{-1}\cdot\sinh(d\gamma^{2})\cdot\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\Big)^{-1}\cdot\frac{\omega_{a}\omega_{a}^{\top}}{\|\omega_{a}\|_{2}^{4}}+C\cdot{\bm{1}}{\bm{1}}^{\top},

(D.22)

where
C_{\gamma}>0
is a number depending
\gamma
.
Noting
that
\omega_{a}
is orthogonal to the all-one vector
{\bm{1}}
, combining (
D.22
), we can simplify (
D.21
) as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\omega_{a})

\displaystyle=1-\Big(1+(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot L^{-1}\cdot\sinh(d\gamma^{2})\cdot\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\Big)^{-1}.

Consequently, minimizing the loss
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\omega_{a})
is equivalent to finding the minimal value of
\sinh(d\gamma^{2})\cdot(d\gamma^{2})^{-1}
.
By simple calculus, we can show that
\sinh(x)/x
is monotonically increasing on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and thus the minimum value of
\sinh(d\gamma^{2})\cdot(d\gamma^{2})^{-1}
is achieved at
\gamma^{*}=0^{+}
.
Then we observe that the loss
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\omega_{a})
is a monotonically increasing function of
\gamma
, and the optimal value of
\omega_{a}
is

\omega_{a}^{*}=(-\gamma^{*},\gamma^{*})
.
Therefore, the optimal value of the loss is given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}^{*}=1-\frac{1}{1+(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot d/L}=\frac{(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot d}{(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot d+L}.

(D.23)

In addition, the optimal choice of
\mu_{a}
and
\mu_{d}
under the limit
\gamma\rightarrow 0^{+}
satisfy

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0^{+}}\langle\mu_{a}^{*}(\omega_{a}^{*}),\omega_{a}^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0^{+}}(\omega_{a}^{*})^{\top}A(\omega_{a}^{*})^{-1}\omega_{a}^{*}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0^{+}}\Big(1+(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot L^{-1}\cdot\sinh(d\gamma^{2})\cdot\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\Big)^{-1}\cdot\frac{((\omega_{a}^{*})^{\top}\omega_{a}^{*})^{2}}{\|\omega_{a}^{*}\|_{2}^{4}}

\displaystyle=\bigl(1+(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot L^{-1}\cdot d\bigr)^{-1},

(D.24)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0^{+}}\mu_{d}^{*}(\mu_{a}^{*})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0^{+}}\langle\mu_{a}^{*},{\bm{1}}\rangle=\bigl(1+(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot L^{-1}\cdot d\bigr)^{-1}\cdot\frac{\langle\omega_{a}^{*},{\bm{1}}\rangle}{\|\omega_{a}^{*}\|_{2}^{4}}=0,

where
\mu_{a}^{*}=A(\omega_{a}^{*})^{-1}\omega_{a}^{*}
is parallel to
(-1,1)
.
Recall that we let
\theta_{\gamma}
denote any element in
\mathscr{S}_{\gamma}
. That is, for any
h\in\mathcal{H}_{+}\cup\mathcal{H}_{-}
,
|\omega^{(h)}|=\gamma
,
\mu_{+}=-\mu_{-}=\mu_{\gamma}
, where
\mu_{\gamma}
is defined in (
4.6
). Moreover, for any dummy head with
h\notin\mathcal{H}_{+}\cup\mathcal{H}_{-}
,
\mu^{(h)}=0
.
Moreover, by the definition of
\mu_{\gamma}
, we have

2\gamma\cdot\mu_{\gamma}=\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\gamma^{2}+(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot L^{-1}\cdot\cosh(d\gamma^{2})}\rightarrow\frac{1}{1+(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot L^{-1}\cdot d}

as
\gamma\rightarrow 0^{+}
.
Therefore, by the construction of
\mu_{a}^{*}
and (
D.24
) we have shown that the global minimizer of
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
over
\bar{\mathscr{S}}
is attained in
\mathscr{S}_{\gamma}
with
\gamma\rightarrow\gamma^{*}=0^{+}
. In other words, the attention model with parameter
(\omega_{a}^{*},\mu_{a}^{*},\mu_{d}^{*})
is exactly the model with parameter
\theta_{\gamma^{*}}
.

Furthermore, recall that we have established an upper bound on the difference between transformer predictor and debiased GD predictor in the proof of Theorem
4.2
-(i).
Let
\eta^{*}
denote
(1+(1+\sigma^{2})\cdot L^{-1}\cdot d\bigr)^{-1}
and let
\eta_{\gamma}=2\gamma\cdot\mu_{\gamma}
.
Given a fixed input
\{(x_{\ell},y_{\ell})\}_{\ell\in[L]}\cup\{x_{q}\}
, as shown in (
D.16
),
for any sufficiently small
\gamma>0
,
we have

|\widehat{y}_{q}(\theta_{\gamma})-\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf gd}(\eta_{\gamma})|\leq\widetilde{O}\bigr(\sqrt{1+\sigma^{2}}\cdot\gamma\cdot d\bigr).

Taking the limit
\gamma\rightarrow\gamma^{*}=0^{+}
,
we prove that the predictor that minimizes the approximate loss
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
over
\mathscr{S}_{\gamma}
,
\widehat{y}_{q}(\theta_{\gamma^{*}})
, coincides with the debiased GD predictor
\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf gd}(\eta_{\gamma}^{*})
.
This completes the proof.
∎

D.3
Proof of Theorem
4.2
-(iii): Bayes Risk

In Theorem
4.2
-(i), we have shown that the difference between the transformer predictor and debiased GD predictor are close when
\theta=(\omega,\mu)\in\bar{\mathscr{S}}
and
\gamma
is small. In the following, we show that the risk of the debiased GD predictor
\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf gd}(\eta^{*})
is comparable to the Bayes optimal predictor, where
\eta^{*}
denotes the optimal learning rate.
This implies that the transformer model approximately learns the Bayes optimal predictor.

Proof of Theorem
4.2
-(iii).

To prove this theorem, we use the vanilla gradient descent predictor as the bridge.
In the first step, we characterize the risk of the vanilla GD predictor. Then, in the second step, we prove that the risk of debiased GD predictor is close to that of the vanilla GD predictor.
Finally, in the last step, we establish the risk of the Bayes optimal predictor.

Step 1: Risk of Vanilla Gradient Descent Predictor.

We define the predictor given by the vanilla gradient descent as follows:

\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf vgd}(\eta)=\frac{\eta}{L}\cdot y^{\top}Xx_{q}=\frac{\eta}{L}\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}y_{\ell}\cdot x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{q},

where
\eta>0
is the learning rate.
We characterize
\mathcal{E}(\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf vgd}(\eta))
, the
\ell_{2}
-risk of vanilla GD predictor as follows. By direct calculation, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\big(\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf vgd}(\eta)-y_{q}\bigr)^{2}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\eta}{L}\cdot y^{\top}Xx_{q}-\beta^{\top}x_{q}\right)^{2}\right]+\sigma^{2}=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left\|\frac{\eta}{L}\cdot X^{\top}(X\beta+\epsilon)-\beta\right\|_{2}^{2}\bigg]+\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left\|\left(\frac{\eta}{L}\cdot X^{\top}X-I_{d}\right)\beta\right\|_{2}^{2}\bigg]}_{\displaystyle\textbf{(i)}}+\frac{\eta^{2}}{L^{2}}\cdot\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left\|X^{\top}\epsilon\right\|_{2}^{2}\bigg]}_{\displaystyle\textbf{(ii)}}+\sigma^{2},

(D.25)

where in the second equality, we take expectation over
x_{q}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
. Furthermore, we have

(i)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\big[\left\|\beta\right\|_{2}^{2}\big]-\frac{2\eta}{L}\cdot\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl\|X\beta\bigl\|_{2}^{2}\bigr]+\frac{\eta^{2}}{L^{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl\|X^{\top}X\beta\bigr\|_{2}^{2}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=1-\frac{2\eta}{L}\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbb{E}[(x_{\ell}^{\top}\beta)^{2}]+\frac{\eta^{2}}{L^{2}}\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbb{E}\left[\|x_{\ell}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot(x_{\ell}^{\top}\beta)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{2\eta^{2}}{L^{2}}\cdot\sum_{1\leq\ell\neq j\leq L}\mathbb{E}\left[x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{j}\cdot\beta^{\top}x_{j}x_{\ell}^{\top}\beta\right],

where we use
\beta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}/d)
.
By moment calculations, we have
\mathbb{E}[(x_{\ell}^{\top}\beta)^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\|\beta\|_{2}^{2}]=1
and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|x_{\ell}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot(x_{\ell}^{\top}\beta)^{2}\right]=\frac{1}{d}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\|x_{\ell}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(x_{\ell}x_{\ell}^{\top})\right]=\frac{1}{d}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\|x_{\ell}\|_{2}^{4}\right]=d+2,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{j}\cdot\beta^{\top}x_{j}x_{\ell}^{\top}\beta\right]=\frac{1}{d}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{j}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(x_{j}x_{\ell}^{\top})\right]=\frac{1}{d}\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[(x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{j})^{2}\big]=1,

for all
1\leq j\neq\ell\leq L
.
Combining the results above, we have

\displaystyle\textbf{(i)}=1-2\eta+\frac{\eta^{2}}{L^{2}}\cdot L(d+2)+\frac{2\eta^{2}}{L^{2}}\cdot\frac{L(L-1)}{2}=1-2\eta+\frac{\eta^{2}}{L}\cdot(d+L+1).

(D.26)

Moreover, based on the i.i.d assumption of the demonstration examples, we have

\displaystyle\textbf{(ii)}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\left\|\epsilon_{\ell}\cdot x_{\ell}\right\|_{2}^{2}\bigr]=L\cdot\mathbb{E}[\|x_{\ell}\|_{2}^{2}]\cdot\mathbb{E}[\epsilon_{\ell}^{2}]=dL\cdot\sigma^{2}.

(D.27)

Combine (
D.25
), (
D.26
) and (
D.27
), the
\ell_{2}
-risk of the vanilla GD predictor is given by

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf vgd}(\eta)-y_{q}\bigr)^{2}\bigr]=1+\sigma^{2}-2\eta+\eta^{2}/L\cdot(d(1+\sigma^{2})+L+1).

This is a quadratic function of
\eta
, and the optimal learning rate that minimizes this risk is given by

\displaystyle\eta^{{\sf vgd},*}=L/(d(1+\sigma^{2})+L+1).

(D.28)

The optimal
\ell_{2}
-risk is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\big(\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf gd}(\eta^{{\sf vgd},*})\big)=1+\sigma^{2}-\frac{L}{d(1+\sigma^{2})+L+1}.

(D.29)

Step 2: Risk of Debiased Gradient Descent Predictor.

Now we compare
\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf gd}(\eta)
and
\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf vgd}(\eta)
in terms of the
\ell_{2}
-risk.
By direct calculation, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf gd}(\eta)-y_{q}\bigr)^{2}\bigr]-\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf vgd}(\eta)-y_{q}\bigr)^{2}\bigr]

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf vgd}(\eta)-y^{\top}{\bm{1}}_{L}\cdot{\bm{1}}_{L}^{\top}Xx_{q}/L^{2}-y_{q}\bigr)^{2}\bigr]-\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf vgd}(\eta)-y_{q}\bigr)^{2}\bigr]

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{L^{4}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\left(y^{\top}{\bm{1}}_{L}\cdot{\bm{1}}_{L}^{\top}Xx_{q}\right)^{2}\right]-\frac{2}{L^{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\eta}{L}\cdot y^{\top}Xx_{q}-\beta^{\top}x_{q}\right)\left(y^{\top}{\bm{1}}_{L}\cdot{\bm{1}}_{L}^{\top}Xx_{q}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{L^{4}}\cdot\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\Big[\bigl\|X^{\top}{\bm{1}}_{L}\cdot{\bm{1}}_{L}^{\top}y\bigr\|_{2}^{2}\Bigr]}_{\displaystyle\textbf{ (iii)}}-\frac{2\eta}{L^{3}}\cdot\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left[y^{\top}{\bm{1}}_{L}\cdot{\bm{1}}_{L}^{\top}XX^{\top}y\right]}_{\displaystyle\textbf{(iv)}}+\frac{2}{L^{2}}\cdot\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left[y^{\top}{\bm{1}}_{L}\cdot{\bm{1}}_{L}^{\top}X\beta\right]}_{\displaystyle\textbf{(v)}}.

In the sequel, we define

\bar{x}_{L}=X^{\top}{\bm{1}}_{L}
. Then,
\bar{x}_{L}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,L\cdot I_{d})
. By direct calculation, we have

(iii)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl\|X^{\top}{\bm{1}}_{L}\cdot{\bm{1}}_{L}^{\top}(X\beta+\epsilon)\bigr\|_{2}^{2}\Bigr]=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl\|X^{\top}{\bm{1}}_{L}\cdot{\bm{1}}_{L}^{\top}X\beta\bigr\|_{2}^{2}\Bigr]+\mathbb{E}\Big[\bigl\|X^{\top}{\bm{1}}_{L}\cdot{\bm{1}}_{L}^{\top}\epsilon\bigr\|_{2}^{2}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl\|\bar{x}_{L}\cdot\bar{x}_{L}^{\top}\beta\bigr\|_{2}^{2}\bigr]+\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\epsilon^{\top}{\bm{1}}_{L})^{2}\bigr]\cdot\mathbb{E}\left\|\bar{x}_{L}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\|\bar{x}_{L}\|_{2}^{4}\right]+dL^{2}\sigma^{2}=L^{2}\cdot(d(1+\sigma^{2})+2).

(D.30)

Moreover, for term
(iv)
, we have

(iv)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[(X\beta+\epsilon)^{\top}{\bm{1}}_{L}\cdot{\bm{1}}_{L}^{\top}XX^{\top}(X\beta+\epsilon)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\beta^{\top}X^{\top}{\bm{1}}_{L}\cdot{\bm{1}}_{L}^{\top}XX^{\top}X\beta\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\epsilon^{\top}{\bm{1}}_{L}\cdot{\bm{1}}_{L}^{\top}XX^{\top}\epsilon\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}\cdot\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl\|XX^{\top}{\bm{1}}_{L}\bigr\|_{2}^{2}\big]+\sigma^{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl\|X^{\top}{\bm{1}}_{L}\bigr\|_{2}^{2}\bigr]=\frac{1}{d}\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbb{E}\bigl[(x_{\ell}^{\top}\bar{x}_{L})^{2}\bigr]+\sigma^{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\|\bar{x}_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\bigr],

where the last equality results from
XX^{\top}{\bm{1}}_{L}=X\bar{x}_{L}
.
Furthermore, by direct calculation, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(x_{\ell}^{\top}\bar{x}_{L})^{2}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\|x_{\ell}\|_{2}^{4}+\sum_{1\leq j\neq\ell\leq L}\mathbb{E}\bigl[(x_{\ell}^{\top}x_{j})^{2}\bigr]+\sum_{1\leq k\neq j\leq L}\mathbb{E}[\|x_{\ell}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot x_{k}^{\top}x_{j}]=d\cdot(d+L+1).

Based on the arguments above, we obtain a closed-form expression of
(iv)
:

\displaystyle\textbf{(iv)}=(d+L+1)\cdot L+dL\cdot\sigma^{2}.

(D.31)

Following a similar argument, for term
(v)
, we have

\displaystyle\textbf{(v)}=\mathbb{E}\bigl[(X\beta+\epsilon)^{\top}{\bm{1}}_{L}\cdot{\bm{1}}_{L}^{\top}X\beta\bigr]=\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\beta^{\top}X^{\top}{\bm{1}}_{L})^{2}\bigr]=\frac{1}{d}\cdot\mathbb{E}\|\bar{x}_{L}\|_{2}^{2}=L.

(D.32)

Combining (
D.30
), (
D.31
) and (
D.32
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf gd}(\eta)-y_{q}\bigr)^{2}\bigr]-\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf vgd}(\eta)-y_{q}\bigr)^{2}\bigr]=\frac{d(1+\sigma^{2})+2+2L}{L^{2}}-\frac{2\eta}{L^{2}}\left(d(1+\sigma^{2})+L+1\right).

When
\eta
is bounded by a constant,
given
L\rightarrow\infty
and
d/L\rightarrow\xi
,
we know that

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf gd}(\eta)-y_{q}\bigr)^{2}\bigr]-\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf vgd}(\eta)-y_{q}\bigr)^{2}\bigr]\rightarrow 0\qquad\textrm{as}~~L\rightarrow\infty~\text{with}~d/L\rightarrow\xi.

Hence, we have
\mathcal{E}(\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf gd}(\eta))\rightarrow\mathcal{E}(\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf vgd}(\eta))
uniformly for all bounded
\eta
.
Thus, under the proportional regime, the optimal learning rate of
\widehat{y}^{\sf gd}(\eta)
coincides with that of
\widehat{y}^{\sf vgd}(\eta)
, which is given by (
D.28
).
Furthermore, the asymptotically optimal learning rate and the limiting
\ell_{2}
risk are given by

\displaystyle\eta^{*}=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}d/L\rightarrow\xi\\
L\rightarrow\infty\end{subarray}}\frac{L}{d(1+\sigma^{2})+L+1}=\frac{1}{\xi\cdot(1+\sigma^{2})+1},

\displaystyle\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}d/L\rightarrow\xi\\
L\rightarrow\infty\end{subarray}}\mathcal{E}(\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf gd}(\eta^{*}))=\sigma^{2}+\frac{\xi\cdot(1+\sigma^{2})}{\xi\cdot(1+\sigma^{2})+1}.

(D.33)

Here we take the limit with
L\rightarrow\infty
and
d/L\rightarrow\xi
in (
D.28
) and (
D.29
) respectively.

Step 3: Risk of Bayes Optimal Predictor.

As a comparison, we also consider the Bayes risk under this setup. Note that under the Gaussian prior over
\beta
, it is a classical result that the Bayesian optimal estimator of
y_{q}
given
\{(x_{\ell},y_{\ell})\}_{\ell\in[L]}\cup{x_{q}}
is given by ridge regression, which takes the form of
\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf ridge}=\widehat{\beta}^{\sf ridge}x_{q}
.
Here, we let
\widehat{\beta}^{\sf ridge,\top}
denote the ridge estimator of
\beta
, and let
\mathsf{BayesRisk}_{\xi,\sigma^{2}}
denote the Bayes risk of
\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf ridge}
, where subscripts
\xi
and
\sigma^{2}
are used to indicate the dependence on
\xi
in the limiting regime and the noise level
\sigma^{2}
.
The ridge estimator and the Bayes risk are given by

\widehat{\beta}^{\sf ridge}=(X^{\top}X+\lambda I_{d})^{-1}X^{\top}y,\qquad\mathsf{BayesRisk}_{\xi,\sigma^{2}}=\mathbb{E}\big[|\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf ridge}-y_{q}|_{2}^{2}\big].

The connection between the ridge estimator and the Bayes optimal estimator is as follows.
With the Gaussian prior
\mathcal{N}(0,\tau^{2}\cdot I_{d})
for some parameter
\tau
, using the Gaussian likelihood of the linear model, we can calculate the posterior distribution of
\beta
. The Bayes optimal estimator of
\beta
is given by the posterior mean, which takes the form of
\widehat{\beta}^{\sf ridge}
with
\lambda=\sigma^{2}/\tau^{2}
.
Thus, in our case where
\beta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}/d)
, the Bayes optimal estimator coincides with the ridge estimator with
\lambda=d\sigma^{2}
.

In the following, we derive the Bayes risk which largely follows the main proof in
Dobriban and Wager (2018)
.
We set
\lambda=d\sigma^{2}
hereafter.
Conditioning on
X
, the risk of
\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf rigde}
is given by

\mathbb{E}[(\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf rigde}-y_{q})^{2}\mid X]=\mathbb{E}[(\widehat{\beta}^{\sf rigde,\top}x_{q}-\beta^{\top}x_{q})^{2}\mid X]+\sigma^{2}=\mathbb{E}[\|\widehat{\beta}^{\sf rigde}-\beta\|_{2}^{2}\mid X]+\sigma^{2},

where the expectation is with respect to the randomness of
x_{q}
,
\epsilon
, and
\beta
.
By direct calculation, we simplify
\mathbb{E}[(\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf rigde}-y_{q})^{2}\mid X]
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\widehat{\beta}^{\sf rigde}-\beta\|_{2}^{2}\mid X]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\|(X^{\top}X+d\sigma^{2}\cdot I_{d})^{-1}X^{\top}(X\beta+\epsilon)-\beta\|_{2}^{2}\mid X]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl\|\bigl((X^{\top}X+d\sigma^{2}\cdot I_{d})^{-1}X^{\top}X-I_{d}\bigr)\beta\bigr\|_{2}^{2}\mid X\bigr]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}[\|(X^{\top}X+d\sigma^{2}\cdot I_{d})^{-1}X^{\top}\epsilon\|_{2}^{2}\mid X]

\displaystyle=d\sigma^{4}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big((X^{\top}X+d\sigma^{2}\cdot I_{d})^{-2}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sigma^{2}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big((X^{\top}X+d\sigma^{2}\cdot I_{d})^{-1}X^{\top}X(X^{\top}X+d\sigma^{2}\cdot I_{d})^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big((X^{\top}X+d\sigma^{2}\cdot I_{d})^{-1}\big),

where the third equality follows from the fact that
(X^{\top}X+d\sigma^{2}I_{d})^{-1}X^{\top}X-I_{d}=d\sigma^{2}\cdot(X^{\top}X+d\sigma^{2}I_{d})^{-1}
, and the last equality is obtained by plugging in
X^{\top}X=(X^{\top}X+d\sigma^{2}\cdot I_{d})-d\sigma^{2}\cdot I_{d}
. By denoting
\xi_{d,L}=d/L
and
\widehat{\Sigma}=X^{\top}X/L
, we can rewrite the equation above as

\displaystyle\sigma^{-2}\cdot\mathbb{E}[\|\widehat{\beta}^{\sf rigde}-\beta\|_{2}^{2}\mid X]=1+\frac{\xi_{d,L}}{d}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big((\widehat{\Sigma}+\sigma^{2}\xi_{d,L}\cdot I_{d})^{-1}\big).

(D.34)

The asymptotic behavior of the trace above can be tracked using random matrix theory
(
Ledoit and Péché, 2011
;
Wang et al., 2024
, e.g.,)
. Let
m_{F}
denote the Stieltjes transform of
F

(
Marchenko and Pastur, 1967
)
, where
F
is the limiting spectral distribution
\widehat{F}(t)=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\ind\{\lambda_{j}(\widehat{\Sigma})\leq t\}
. Then,

\displaystyle\frac{\xi_{d,L}}{d}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\big((\widehat{\Sigma}+\xi_{d,L}\cdot I_{d})^{-1}\big)\overset{\rm a.s.}{\longrightarrow}\xi\cdot m_{F}(-\sigma^{2}\xi)\text{~~with~~}\xi_{d,L}\rightarrow\xi.

(D.35)

Under the isotropic case where
x_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
,
m_{F}
has an explicit expression

m_{F}(t)=(1-\xi-t-\sqrt{(1-\xi-t)^{2}-4\xi t})/2\xi t

for all
t\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
Based on (
D.34
) and (
D.35
), we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{BayesRisk}_{\xi,\sigma^{2}}\overset{\rm a.s.}{\longrightarrow}\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(\sigma^{2}+1-{\xi}^{-1}+\sqrt{4\sigma^{2}+\left(\sigma^{2}+{\xi}^{-1}-1\right)^{2}}\Bigr).

(D.36)

Combining (
D.33
) and (
D.36
), the excess risk of
\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf gd}(\eta^{*})
compared to the Bayes risk satisfies that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf gd}(\eta^{*}))-\mathsf{BayesRisk}_{\xi,\sigma^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad=\sigma^{2}+1-\frac{1}{\xi(1+\sigma^{2})+1}-\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(\sigma^{2}+1-{\xi}^{-1}\Bigr)-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{4\sigma^{2}+(\sigma^{2}+\xi^{-1}-1)^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{2}\big(\sigma^{2}+1+\xi^{-1}-\sqrt{4\sigma^{2}+(\sigma^{2}+\xi^{-1}-1)^{2}}\big)-(\xi(1+\sigma^{2})+1)^{-1}

\displaystyle\quad=2\xi^{-1}\cdot\big(\sigma^{2}+\xi^{-1}+1+\sqrt{4\sigma^{2}+(\sigma^{2}+\xi^{-1}-1)^{2}}\big)^{-1}-(\xi(1+\sigma^{2})+1)^{-1}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\xi^{-1}\cdot\{(1+\xi\sigma^{2})\cdot(1+\sigma^{2}+\xi^{-1})\}^{-1}

where the inequality results from
\sqrt{4\sigma^{2}+(\sigma^{2}+\xi^{-1}-1)^{2}}\geq\sigma^{2}+\xi^{-1}-1
since we assume
\sigma^{2}+\xi^{-1}\geq 1
.
Furthermore, since
\mathsf{BayesRisk}_{\xi,\sigma^{2}}\geq\sigma^{2}
and we assume
\sigma^{2}>0
, then it holds that

\frac{\mathcal{E}(\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf gd}(\eta^{*}))}{\mathsf{BayesRisk}_{\xi,\sigma^{2}}}\leq 1+2\sigma^{-2}\cdot\{(1+\xi\sigma^{2})\cdot(1+\sigma^{2}+\xi^{-1})\}^{-1},

which gives that
\widehat{y}_{q}^{\sf gd}(\eta^{*})
is near optimal when
\xi
or
\sigma^{2}
is large and then we complete the proof.
∎

Appendix E
Proof of Technical Lemmas

E.1
Proof of Lemma
C.2

Proof of Lemma
C.2
